It’s more like I’m falling behind on normal things. I missed an internal lab deadline because I just couldn’t get my writing and data review organized, and after that even stuff like preparing reports feels harder to keep up with.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve skipped art nights a few times, and cooking for my family has started to feel like… a lot. I still want to do those things, I’m just not keeping up very well. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.0, F48.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thanks for seeing me. I guess I’m here because I’ve been worrying a lot—probably more than I should. My mind keeps jumping between things, like whether the new partnership at the lab is going to mean my position gets cut, or whether my data is wrong, or if my bills are going to pile up. And then I start thinking my family probably thinks I’m falling behind, and that I may have already messed up my chances at grad school. It’s just… a lot, and I’m not handling it as well as I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think I’ve been avoiding things, exactly. Or at least I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern like that. I still try to do what I’m supposed to do. Maybe I hesitate sometimes, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say sad most of the time, no. I mean, I get frustrated and kind of worn out by everything, but… sad or down all day, most days? I haven’t really noticed it like that. Is that okay—like, is that the distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep patterns lately?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I haven’t been tracking it or anything. I guess my sleep hasn’t felt great, but I don’t know if it’s a real “pattern” or just normal stress, if that makes sense. Is that the kind of change you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches or stomachaches lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I mean, I wouldn’t want to make it sound dramatic, but my shoulders and neck are tight a lot during the day. I notice it when I’m working on spreadsheets—I’ll be clenching my jaw without realizing it. By the evening I can get headaches and feel really stiff, kind of like I’ve been carrying equipment around all day, even if I haven’t. Does that count as the kind of physical symptom you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been pulling back from social interactions or spending less time with friends and family lately?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… though I don’t want to make it sound like I’m ignoring people on purpose. It’s more like I’m falling behind on normal things. I missed an internal lab deadline because I just couldn’t get my writing and data review organized, and after that even stuff like preparing reports feels harder to keep up with.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve skipped art nights a few times, and cooking for my family has started to feel like… a lot. I still want to do those things, I’m just not keeping up very well. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I try not to make a big deal of it, but at work I feel kind of wound up, like I can’t really settle into my chair. I’ll catch myself tapping my foot, checking messages, or getting up and walking back to the instrument room even when I don’t really need anything.\n\nAnd sometimes normal lab noises make me jump more than they probably should. I don’t know if that’s what you mean by “on edge,” but… yeah, I guess that’s been happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're disconnected from yourself or your surroundings?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Does that make sense?\n\n[Last Question]\nAre your worries mostly about one area of your life, like work, or do they cover a wide range of things?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 24 / chemist_or_materials_scientist / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Excessive_Anxiety_Worry\nmanifestation: \"I came in because my mind is constantly jumping from whether our lab's new partnership is going to cut my position to whether my data are wrong, my bills are going to pile up, my family thinks I'm falling behind, and whether I've already ruined my chances at grad school.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s… not just work, I guess. Work is the easiest thing to point to, because it’s right there every day, but it kind of spreads.

I worry about the lab’s new partnership and whether that means my position could get cut, and then I start wondering if my data are wrong, and then it jumps to bills piling up, or my family thinking I’m falling behind. And then I start thinking I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school somehow.

I know that probably sounds like a lot, but it feels like my mind doesn’t stay in one lane. It moves from one thing to the next pretty fast.

I mean, I get frustrated and kind of worn out by everything, but… sad or down all day, most days? I haven’t really noticed it like that. Is that okay—like, is that the distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep patterns lately?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I haven’t been tracking it or anything. I guess my sleep hasn’t felt great, but I don’t know if it’s a real “pattern” or just normal stress, if that makes sense. Is that the kind of change you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches or stomachaches lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I mean, I wouldn’t want to make it sound dramatic, but my shoulders and neck are tight a lot during the day. I still want to do those things, I’m just not keeping up very well. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I try not to make a big deal of it, but at work I feel kind of wound up, like I can’t really settle into my chair.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if there’s one single thing at the center… I wish I could say it was just work or just money or something clear like that.

It’s more like my mind keeps jumping around. I came in because I’ll start worrying about whether the lab’s new partnership could mean my position gets cut, and then I’m worrying my data might be wrong, and then it’s bills piling up, or my family thinking I’m falling behind, or that I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school.

You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… if I’m being honest, it’s not one neat fear. I kind of wish it were, because that would sound more reasonable.

It’s like I came in because my mind keeps jumping from thing to thing — whether the lab’s new partnership is going to mean my position gets cut, then whether my data are wrong, then bills piling up, then my family thinking I’m falling behind, and then whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school.

So maybe the bigger concern underneath is that I’m not keeping up, or that I’m going to lose whatever stability I have. But it doesn’t really stay on one specific fear for long, if that makes sense.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… not in a specific way, at least.

Like, I don’t have a thing I feel I have to do to make the worry go away, if that’s what you mean. I might think it over a lot, but I’m not sure I’d call it an urge to perform something. Maybe I’m missing what you mean, though. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Mostly everyday things, I guess — work, money, whether I’m doing things right.

But it also kind of turns into bigger stuff pretty quickly. Like I came in because my mind keeps jumping from whether the lab’s new partnership could cut my position, to whether my data are wrong, to bills piling up, to my family thinking I’m falling behind… and then it becomes this bigger fear that I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school or my future.

So, I don’t know if that counts as abstract, but it’s not just one practical concern. It kind of spreads. Is that what you mean?

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say they’re completely unrealistic, because there’s usually some real thing underneath them. But they definitely get… bigger than they need to.

Like I’ll try to tell myself, “Just focus on this one report,” or “Just check this assay,” and then one worry sets off another. Pretty soon I’m just staring at the screen, and my head is running through every possible way things could go wrong.

I know that probably isn’t productive, and I can see that in the moment sometimes, but it’s hard to stop once it starts. Does that make sense?

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.0, F48.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thanks for seeing me. I guess I’m here because I’ve been worrying a lot—probably more than I should. My mind keeps jumping between things, like whether the new partnership at the lab is going to mean my position gets cut, or whether my data is wrong, or if my bills are going to pile up. And then I start thinking my family probably thinks I’m falling behind, and that I may have already messed up my chances at grad school. It’s just… a lot, and I’m not handling it as well as I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think I’ve been avoiding things, exactly. Or at least I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern like that. I still try to do what I’m supposed to do. Maybe I hesitate sometimes, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say sad most of the time, no. I mean, I get frustrated and kind of worn out by everything, but… sad or down all day, most days? I haven’t really noticed it like that. Is that okay—like, is that the distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep patterns lately?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I haven’t been tracking it or anything. I guess my sleep hasn’t felt great, but I don’t know if it’s a real “pattern” or just normal stress, if that makes sense. Is that the kind of change you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches or stomachaches lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I mean, I wouldn’t want to make it sound dramatic, but my shoulders and neck are tight a lot during the day. I notice it when I’m working on spreadsheets—I’ll be clenching my jaw without realizing it. By the evening I can get headaches and feel really stiff, kind of like I’ve been carrying equipment around all day, even if I haven’t. Does that count as the kind of physical symptom you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been pulling back from social interactions or spending less time with friends and family lately?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… though I don’t want to make it sound like I’m ignoring people on purpose. It’s more like I’m falling behind on normal things. I missed an internal lab deadline because I just couldn’t get my writing and data review organized, and after that even stuff like preparing reports feels harder to keep up with.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve skipped art nights a few times, and cooking for my family has started to feel like… a lot. I still want to do those things, I’m just not keeping up very well. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I try not to make a big deal of it, but at work I feel kind of wound up, like I can’t really settle into my chair. I’ll catch myself tapping my foot, checking messages, or getting up and walking back to the instrument room even when I don’t really need anything.\n\nAnd sometimes normal lab noises make me jump more than they probably should. I don’t know if that’s what you mean by “on edge,” but… yeah, I guess that’s been happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're disconnected from yourself or your surroundings?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Not in a way I’ve really noticed, at least. Sometimes I’m distracted or kind of in my head, but I wouldn’t say I feel like I’m outside myself or like things aren’t real.\n\nIs that what you mean, or is there a specific kind of feeling I should be looking for?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have panic attacks or feel like you're having a panic attack?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d call them panic attacks, no. I mean, I get really wound up sometimes, but I haven’t had, like… a sudden episode where I felt like I was dying or totally out of control.\n\nUnless I’m defining it too narrowly? But as far as I can tell, no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and feel like you can't stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but yes. I’ll try to tell myself, like, “Just focus on this one thing,” whether it’s finishing a report or checking an assay, and then one worry kind of leads into another.\n\nPretty soon I’m just staring at the screen, and my head is running through every possible way something could go wrong. I can sometimes pull myself back, but it doesn’t feel very easy to stop once it starts. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat certain actions or rituals to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so, not really. I mean, I might double-check things sometimes, but I feel like that’s kind of normal with lab work, you know? I wouldn’t say I have rituals or that I have to repeat something in a specific way to calm down.\n\nUnless you mean something broader than that? But nothing obvious comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries are interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think they are. I don’t love admitting that, because I’m usually pretty good at keeping things together, but it’s been affecting stuff.\n\nI missed an internal lab deadline recently because I just couldn’t get my writing and data review organized. I kept circling around it and second-guessing what to include, and then I just… didn’t finish in time.\n\nAnd it’s not only work. Even routine things feel harder than they should, like preparing reports, or showing up to art nights I normally enjoy, or cooking for my family. I can still do some of it, but it feels like I’m barely keeping up. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are your worries mostly about one area of your life, like work, or do they cover a wide range of things?\n\nPatient: It’s… not just work, I guess. Work is the easiest thing to point to, because it’s right there every day, but it kind of spreads.\n\nI worry about the lab’s new partnership and whether that means my position could get cut, and then I start wondering if my data are wrong, and then it jumps to bills piling up, or my family thinking I’m falling behind. And then I start thinking I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school somehow.\n\nI know that probably sounds like a lot, but it feels like my mind doesn’t stay in one lane. It moves from one thing to the next pretty fast.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular worry or fear that seems to be at the center of all your other concerns?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if there’s one single thing at the center… I wish I could say it was just work or just money or something clear like that.\n\nIt’s more like my mind keeps jumping around. I came in because I’ll start worrying about whether the lab’s new partnership could mean my position gets cut, and then I’m worrying my data might be wrong, and then it’s bills piling up, or my family thinking I’m falling behind, or that I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school.\n\nSo… maybe the center is that I feel like I’m falling behind or losing control, but it doesn’t stay on one fear for very long. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular fear or concern that you think is driving most of your worries?\n\nPatient: I think… if I’m being honest, it’s not one neat fear. I kind of wish it were, because that would sound more reasonable.\n\nIt’s like I came in because my mind keeps jumping from thing to thing — whether the lab’s new partnership is going to mean my position gets cut, then whether my data are wrong, then bills piling up, then my family thinking I’m falling behind, and then whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school.\n\nSo maybe the bigger concern underneath is that I’m not keeping up, or that I’m going to lose whatever stability I have. But it doesn’t really stay on one specific fear for long, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific mental acts or behaviors in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a specific way, at least.\n\nLike, I don’t have a thing I feel I have to do to make the worry go away, if that’s what you mean. I might think it over a lot, but I’m not sure I’d call it an urge to perform something. Maybe I’m missing what you mean, though. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Are your worries mostly about everyday things, like work or finances, or do they also include more abstract concerns, such as your worth as a person or the future in general?\n\nPatient: Mostly everyday things, I guess — work, money, whether I’m doing things right.\n\nBut it also kind of turns into bigger stuff pretty quickly. It kind of spreads. It’s just… a lot, and I’m not handling it as well as I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think I’ve been avoiding things, exactly. I still want to do those things, I’m just not keeping up very well. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not in a way I’ve really noticed, at least. I mean, I get really wound up sometimes, but I haven’t had, like… a sudden episode where I felt like I was dying or totally out of control.\n\nUnless I’m defining it too narrowly? Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat certain actions or rituals to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so, not really. I mean, I might double-check things sometimes, but I feel like that’s kind of normal with lab work, you know? I wouldn’t say I have rituals or that I have to repeat something in a specific way to calm down.\n\nUnless you mean something broader than that? But nothing obvious comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries are interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think they are. I don’t love admitting that, because I’m usually pretty good at keeping things together, but it’s been affecting stuff.\n\nI missed an internal lab deadline recently because I just couldn’t get my writing and data review organized. I kept circling around it and second-guessing what to include, and then I just… didn’t finish in time.\n\nAnd it’s not only work. Even routine things feel harder than they should, like preparing reports, or showing up to art nights I normally enjoy, or cooking for my family. I can still do some of it, but it feels like I’m barely keeping up. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are your worries mostly about one area of your life, like work, or do they cover a wide range of things?\n\nPatient: It’s… not just work, I guess. Work is the easiest thing to point to, because it’s right there every day, but it kind of spreads.\n\nI worry about the lab’s new partnership and whether that means my position could get cut, and then I start wondering if my data are wrong, and then it jumps to bills piling up, or my family thinking I’m falling behind. And then I start thinking I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school somehow.\n\nI know that probably sounds like a lot, but it feels like my mind doesn’t stay in one lane. It moves from one thing to the next pretty fast.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular worry or fear that seems to be at the center of all your other concerns?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if there’s one single thing at the center… I wish I could say it was just work or just money or something clear like that.\n\nIt’s more like my mind keeps jumping around. I came in because I’ll start worrying about whether the lab’s new partnership could mean my position gets cut, and then I’m worrying my data might be wrong, and then it’s bills piling up, or my family thinking I’m falling behind, or that I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school.\n\nSo… maybe the center is that I feel like I’m falling behind or losing control, but it doesn’t stay on one fear for very long. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular fear or concern that you think is driving most of your worries?\n\nPatient: I think… if I’m being honest, it’s not one neat fear. I kind of wish it were, because that would sound more reasonable.\n\nIt’s like I came in because my mind keeps jumping from thing to thing — whether the lab’s new partnership is going to mean my position gets cut, then whether my data are wrong, then bills piling up, then my family thinking I’m falling behind, and then whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school.\n\nSo maybe the bigger concern underneath is that I’m not keeping up, or that I’m going to lose whatever stability I have. I’ll try to tell myself, like, “Just focus on this one thing,” whether it’s finishing a report or checking an assay, and then one worry kind of leads into another.\n\nPretty soon I’m just staring at the screen, and my head is running through every possible way something could go wrong. I can sometimes pull myself back, but it doesn’t feel very easy to stop once it starts. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat certain actions or rituals to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so, not really. I mean, I might double-check things sometimes, but I feel like that’s kind of normal with lab work, you know? I wouldn’t say I have rituals or that I have to repeat something in a specific way to calm down.\n\nUnless you mean something broader than that? But nothing obvious comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries are interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think they are. I don’t love admitting that, because I’m usually pretty good at keeping things together, but it’s been affecting stuff.\n\nI missed an internal lab deadline recently because I just couldn’t get my writing and data review organized. I kept circling around it and second-guessing what to include, and then I just… didn’t finish in time.\n\nAnd it’s not only work. Even routine things feel harder than they should, like preparing reports, or showing up to art nights I normally enjoy, or cooking for my family. I can still do some of it, but it feels like I’m barely keeping up. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are your worries mostly about one area of your life, like work, or do they cover a wide range of things?\n\nPatient: It’s… not just work, I guess. Work is the easiest thing to point to, because it’s right there every day, but it kind of spreads.\n\nI worry about the lab’s new partnership and whether that means my position could get cut, and then I start wondering if my data are wrong, and then it jumps to bills piling up, or my family thinking I’m falling behind. And then I start thinking I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school somehow.\n\nI know that probably sounds like a lot, but it feels like my mind doesn’t stay in one lane. It moves from one thing to the next pretty fast.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular worry or fear that seems to be at the center of all your other concerns?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if there’s one single thing at the center… I wish I could say it was just work or just money or something clear like that.\n\nIt’s more like my mind keeps jumping around. I came in because I’ll start worrying about whether the lab’s new partnership could mean my position gets cut, and then I’m worrying my data might be wrong, and then it’s bills piling up, or my family thinking I’m falling behind, or that I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school.\n\nSo… maybe the center is that I feel like I’m falling behind or losing control, but it doesn’t stay on one fear for very long. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular fear or concern that you think is driving most of your worries?\n\nPatient: I think… if I’m being honest, it’s not one neat fear. I kind of wish it were, because that would sound more reasonable.\n\nIt’s like I came in because my mind keeps jumping from thing to thing — whether the lab’s new partnership is going to mean my position gets cut, then whether my data are wrong, then bills piling up, then my family thinking I’m falling behind, and then whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school.\n\nSo maybe the bigger concern underneath is that I’m not keeping up, or that I’m going to lose whatever stability I have. But it doesn’t really stay on one specific fear for long, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific mental acts or behaviors in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a specific way, at least.\n\nLike, I don’t have a thing I feel I have to do to make the worry go away, if that’s what you mean. I might think it over a lot, but I’m not sure I’d call it an urge to perform something. Maybe I’m missing what you mean, though. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Are your worries mostly about everyday things, like work or finances, or do they also include more abstract concerns, such as your worth as a person or the future in general?\n\nPatient: Mostly everyday things, I guess — work, money, whether I’m doing things right.\n\nBut it also kind of turns into bigger stuff pretty quickly. Like I came in because my mind keeps jumping from whether the lab’s new partnership could cut my position, to whether my data are wrong, to bills piling up, to my family thinking I’m falling behind… and then it becomes this bigger fear that I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school or my future.\n\nSo, I don’t know if that counts as abstract, but it’s not just one practical concern. It kind of spreads. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like any of your worries are completely unrealistic or irrational, but you can't stop thinking about them anyway?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say they’re completely unrealistic, because there’s usually some real thing underneath them. But they definitely get… bigger than they need to.\n\nLike I’ll try to tell myself, “Just focus on this one report,” or “Just check this assay,” and then one worry sets off another. Pretty soon I’m just staring at the screen, and my head is running through every possible way things could go wrong.\n\nI know that probably isn’t productive, and I can see that in the moment sometimes, but it’s hard to stop once it starts. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have your worries been causing problems in your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m impossible to be around or anything. But I’ve been letting people down more than I’d like.\n\nI missed an internal lab deadline because I just couldn’t get my writing and data review organized, and then outside of work it kind of spills over. Like even normal things — preparing reports, showing up for art nights with friends, cooking for my family — they start feeling like too much to keep up with.\n\nI think people are trying to be understanding, but I worry I’m disappointing them. Does that answer what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
